id: "01.13"
course: "ai-research-track-web"
module: "01"
slug: "singular-value-decomposition-svd"
title: "Singular Value Decomposition (SVD)"
question: "A 4096×4096 weight update has singular values that drop to near zero after the first 16. What does that tell you, and which method exploits it?"
answer: "The update is approximately rank 16: it can be written as <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6833em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0502em;\">B</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">A</span></span></span></span> with <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6833em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0502em;\">B</span></span></span></span> of shape 4096×16 and <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6833em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">A</span></span></span></span> 16×4096, about 131k numbers instead of 16.8M. LoRA trains exactly such a low-rank update; truncated SVD of the full update gives the best rank-16 approximation (Eckart–Young)."
sources: [{"title": "Gilbert Strang: Singular Value Decomposition (MIT 18.06)", "url": "https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TX_vooSnhm8", "note": "The classic lecture."}, {"title": "Visual Kernel: SVD visualized", "url": "https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=vSczTbgc8Rc", "note": "The rotate-stretch-rotate picture, animated."}, {"title": "NumPy: linalg.svd", "url": "https://numpy.org/doc/stable/reference/generated/numpy.linalg.svd.html", "note": "Note that it returns V transposed."}, {"title": "3Blue1Brown, Essence of Linear Algebra (all 16 chapters)", "url": "https://www.3blue1brown.com/topics/linear-algebra", "note": "About 3 h"}, {"title": "Fanout 01.03 Vectors, 01.05 Matrices, 01.10 Hadamard product (free)", "url": "https://fanout.sh/ai/lessons/math-fundamentals-matrices", "note": ""}, {"title": "Mathematics for Machine Learning, ch. 2-4", "url": "https://mml-book.github.io/", "note": "Ch. 4 (decompositions) is the most important"}, {"title": "Gilbert Strang, MIT 18.06SC Linear Algebra: lectures 1-10, 14-17, 21-22, 29-30", "url": "https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-06sc-linear-algebra-fall-2011/", "note": ""}, {"title": "Strang, MIT 18.065 Matrix Methods in Data Analysis and ML: lectures 1-9", "url": "https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-065-matrix-methods-in-data-analysis-signal-processing-and-machine-learning-spring-2018/", "note": "SVD, low rank, norms; directly ML-oriented"}]
html: "<section class=\"art\"><h2>Singular value decomposition: every matrix is rotate, stretch, rotate</h2><p class=\"cite\"><span class=\"lab\">Based on</span> Strang, Introduction to Linear Algebra ch. 7 · Trefethen and Bau, Numerical Linear Algebra lectures 4–5 · Hu et al., &quot;LoRA&quot; (2021) · Fanout 01.13</p><h3>Why this matters</h3>\n<ul>\n<li><strong>LoRA</strong>, the standard way to fine-tune big models cheaply, relies on weight updates being approximately low-rank. SVD is how you measure rank.</li>\n<li><strong>PCA</strong>, recommender systems and image compression are all SVD.</li>\n<li>When people study &quot;what a layer has learned&quot;, they often look at its singular values.</li>\n</ul>\n<h3>The idea in plain words</h3>\n<p>Lesson 01.05 showed that a matrix moves every point of the plane. Here is the surprising fact: <strong>any</strong> matrix, however messy, does just three simple things in a row:</p>\n<ol>\n<li><strong>Rotate</strong> (or reflect) the input. That is <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.8491em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\">V</span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.8491em;\"><span style=\"top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\">⊤</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>.</li>\n<li><strong>Stretch</strong> along the axes, by amounts <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.786em;vertical-align:-0.15em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" picture, compressed by keeping only the first <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0315em;\">k</span></span></span></span> singular values. The bars below are all 24 singular values; the teal ones are kept. With a handful you already recognise the shape.</figcaption></figure>\n<h3>In code</h3>\n<pre class=\"code\"><code class=\"language-python\">import numpy as np\nA = np.array([[3., 0.], [4., 5.]])\nU, s, Vt = np.linalg.svd(A)     # s = [6.708, 2.236]\nnp.allclose(U @ np.diag(s) @ Vt, A)   # True\n\n# best rank-k approximation\nk = 1\nA_k = U[:, :k] @ np.diag(s[:k]) @ Vt[:k, :]\n</code></pre>\n<p>In PyTorch: <code>torch.linalg.svd(A, full_matrices=False)</code>.</p>\n<h3>Common mistakes</h3>\n<ul>\n<li><strong>Forgetting NumPy returns <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.8491em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\">V</span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.8491em;\"><span style=\"top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\">⊤</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>, not <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6833em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\">V</span></span></span></span>.</strong> The rows of <code>Vt</code> are the right singular vectors.</li>\n<li><strong>Confusing singular values with eigenvalues.</strong> They match only for symmetric matrices with non-negative eigenvalues. Singular values are never negative; eigenvalues can be.</li>\n<li><strong>Running a full SVD on a huge matrix.</strong> It costs about <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1.0641em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0278em;\">O</span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">m</span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">n</span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.8141em;\"><span style=\"top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\">2</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span></span></span></span>. Use <code>torch.svd_lowrank</code> or randomised SVD when you only need the top few.</li>\n<li><strong>Deciding rank by &quot;exactly zero&quot;.</strong> Floating point gives <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.8141em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">1</span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord\">0</span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.8141em;\"><span style=\"top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\">−</span><span class=\"mord mtight\">16</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>, not 0. Use a tolerance.</li>\n</ul>\n<h3>Try it yourself</h3>\n<ol>\n<li>What are the singular values of <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:2.4em;vertical-align:-0.95em;\"></span><span class=\"minner\"><span class=\"mopen delimcenter\" style=\"top:0em;\"><span class=\"delimsizing size3\">(</span></span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mtable\"><span class=\"col-align-c\"><span class=\"vlist-t vlist-t2\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:1.45em;\"><span style=\"top:-3.61em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:3em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord\">0</span></span></span><span style=\"top:-2.41em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:3em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord\">−</span><span class=\"mord\">3</span></span></span></span><span class=\"vlist-s\">​</span></span><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.95em;\"><span></span></span></span></span></span><span class=\"arraycolsep\" style=\"width:0.5em;\"></span><span class=\"arraycolsep\" style=\"width:0.5em;\"></span><span class=\"col-align-c\"><span class=\"vlist-t vlist-t2\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:1.45em;\"><span style=\"top:-3.61em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:3em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord\">3</span></span></span><span style=\"top:-2.41em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:3em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord\">0</span></span></span></span><span class=\"vlist-s\">​</span></span><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.95em;\"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class=\"mclose delimcenter\" style=\"top:0em;\"><span class=\"delimsizing size3\">)</span></span></span></span></span></span>? (Hint: what does it do to the circle?)</li>\n<li>A <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.7278em;vertical-align:-0.0833em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">512</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span><span class=\"mbin\">×</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6444em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">512</span></span></span></span> matrix has rank 4. How many numbers do you need to store its SVD exactly?</li>\n<li>Why does LoRA pick a small rank <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0278em;\">r</span></span></span></span> rather than, say, 2048?</li>\n</ol>\n<details><summary>Answers</summary><ol>\n<li>It rotates by 90° and scales by 3, so the circle becomes a circle of radius 3: <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.7278em;vertical-align:-0.0833em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">96</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span><span class=\"mbin\">×</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6444em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">96</span></span></span></span> image and its best rank-1, rank-3 and rank-10 approximations from the truncated SVD. The right panel shows why this works: the singular values fall by two orders of magnitude within the first 20, and the tail is mostly noise.</figcaption></figure>\n<p>Real weight updates and many real data matrices have fast-decaying spectra. This is the empirical bet LoRA makes: the change needed during fine-tuning lives in a low-dimensional subspace, so <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6833em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">Δ</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.1389em;\">W</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">=</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6833em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0502em;\">B</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">A</span></span></span></span> with small <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0278em;\">r</span></span></span></span> loses little. <strong>PCA</strong> is the same theorem applied to a centred data matrix: the top right singular vectors are the directions of greatest variance, and projecting onto them is the best linear compression. Goodfellow et al. §2.12 derive PCA from scratch as exactly this optimisation, and MML chapter 10 derives it three ways.</p>\n<h3>Randomized SVD</h3>\n<p>For a large matrix you rarely need all singular vectors. Strang&#39;s 18.065 and the Halko-Martinsson-Tropp method go like this:</p>\n<ol>\n<li>Multiply <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6833em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">A</span></span></span></span> by a random <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6667em;vertical-align:-0.0833em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">n</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span><span class=\"mbin\">×</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0315em;\">k</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span><span class=\"mbin\">+</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span></span></span></span> Gaussian matrix to get a sketch <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6833em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\">Y</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">=</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6833em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">A</span><span class=\"mord\">Ω</span></span></span></span>. Its columns span roughly the top-<span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0315em;\">k</span></span></span></span> column space; <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6776em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">≈</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6444em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">5</span></span></span></span>-10 extra columns add safety.</li>\n<li>Optionally sharpen with a couple of power iterations, <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6833em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\">Y</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">=</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1.0991em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">A</span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">A</span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.8491em;\"><span style=\"top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\">⊤</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class=\"mclose\"><span class=\"mclose\">)</span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.6644em;\"><span style=\"top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal mtight\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">q</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">A</span><span class=\"mord\">Ω</span></span></span></span>, which raises each singular value to the power <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span lead: "Lesson 01.05 showed that a matrix moves every point of the plane. Here is the surprising fact: <strong>any</strong> matrix, however messy, does just three simple things in a row:"
